✻ Crunched for 34s

❯ you need to focus on what I am telling you and not second guess me            

● Understood. Taking it as fact: section1 is gone.

  My analysis:

  The staged session is a bad idea. The whole pitch of this project is a record
  a human can inspect. A fabricated session is a fake entry in that record, and
  one fake entry poisons the rest of it. If a reader ever notices the seams, the
  trust argument of every post dies with it. I also won't help present a staged
  transcript as a real one.

  Confession is cheaper than it feels. Section1 is the official Screeps
  tutorial's own JS — the upstream source is public and stable. The reference
  copy was always just a snapshot of someone else's published code. What readers
  inspect is the dox-populi side: the spec, the shell, the git history of the
  port. Losing the upstream snapshot costs the story very little.

  So two honest options:

  1. Recreate the snapshot and say so. Re-copy section1 from the official
  tutorial into the reference repo, with one plain sentence wherever it matters:
  "the original section1 snapshot was lost; this copy was recreated from the
  official tutorial." Done.
  2. Run the session for real. If you want a genuine session record for
  section1, we do the work now and date it now. It's a real record with a late
  date, and the step-by-step claim stays true.

  My recommendation is option 1. The step-by-step evidence lives in dox-populi's
  history, which you still have.

❯ the problem is this is not exactly reproducable because you are starting with 
  the final result of the project as context. this is not how real projects     
  work. it's allowed to provide a degree of artifice in an educational project, 
  but pretending it's reproducable is not helpful                               

● Agreed. A redo done today is written with the finished architecture in hand —
  the brain/hands split, the spec conventions, the harness shape all exist and
  will leak into it. The original section 1 was worked out without any of that.
  So the redo is a worked example, and calling it a reproduction of the original
  process would be false.

  That gives you the honest label: sections 2–5 have real session records from
  when the architecture was being worked out. Section 1 is a worked example,
  produced later, with the final architecture already known. Every textbook does
  this — the cleaned-up derivation is standard educational artifice, and
  readers accept it as long as it's labeled. One sentence in the section 1 post
  does the labeling, and the reproducibility claim attaches only to what
  actually reproduces: a reader following the section 1 example gets working
  code through the same checkers. The claim you drop is that this is how the
  work first happened.

  The alternative — engineering a "clean room" session that hides the final repo
  from me — chases a purity it can't reach (the conventions are in the agent
  definitions, the memory, this conversation) and buys nothing the label
  doesn't.

  So my verdict: do section 1 for real, present it as a hindsight worked
  example, keep the discovery story attached to the sections that have real
  records.

❯ the finished arch is the main branch, tutorial 1 was supposed to be the       
  minimal first step that completes tutorial 1. we can't do that and the main   
  branch is the finished arch.                                                  

● The bind is real for a fresh rewrite. But there's a version that doesn't start
  from main: the repo's earliest commit is a period artifact. It's the state
  section 2 started from, which means it contains the section-1-complete
  baseline plus whatever section 2 had added by that point — and its
  architecture is the early one, whatever that was at the time, since main's
  finished shape came later.

  So instead of writing section 1 forward from the finished arch, derive it
  backward from that first commit: strip out the section 2 additions and what
  remains is the base the real project actually stood on when section 1 was
  done. The hindsight involved is subtraction from a real artifact. Then two
  pieces of evidence attach to it, both inspectable: the tutorial-1 itest
  witnesses that this baseline completes tutorial 1, and the diff from this
  baseline to the real earliest commit shows it sits strictly inside the
  recorded history.

  The honest claim that survives is "this is the base the recorded history is
  consistent with, and it completes tutorial 1." The claim that dies is "this is
  the minimal first step as it was originally found" — that record is gone and
  nothing rebuilds it. The post says so in one sentence.

  If the earliest commit already carries too much later structure to subtract
  cleanly, then the derivation fails on inspection and the fallback is the other
  honest shape: the blog starts at section 2 and says the section 1 record
  wasn't kept.

❯ tell me what I need to do to deliver the code and see it running              

● Checking the flake for the exact commands before I answer.
  Read 1 file (ctrl+o to expand)

● This baseline's flake deploys to screeps.com. Two commands:

  1. nix flake check — Paradox checks the spec, tsc typechecks the harness
  against the regenerated decision logic, and the build bundles main.js. This is
  where the spec edit gets verified.
  2. nix run .#deploy — rebuilds main.js from the current spec and harness,
  decrypts the Screeps token with ~/.ssh/gitlab, and pushes to branch default on
  screeps.com.

  To see it running: open your room at screeps.com. The harness spawns
  harvester-<tick> on its own as soon as the spawn is free and no creep exists,
  then you watch it cycle between the source and Spawn1.

  If you also want your editor to see the regenerated types locally, nix run 
  .#generate rewrites ./generated and ./vendor.

✻ Crunched for 11s

❯ si there is no itest in this branch? what is being tested when i run nix      
  flake check                                                                   

● Correct — this branch has no itest. nix flake check here runs three checks,
  from the flake's checks block:

  1. paradox-check — runs paradox check on dox/. Paradox (with Z3) verifies the
  spec's named properties and rejects a malformed spec.
  2. typecheck — runs tsc --noEmit over the assembled project: the harness, the
  decision logic freshly generated from the spec, and the vendored Screeps
  types. A harness call that disagrees with the generated types is a compile
  error.
  3. build — esbuild bundles harness/main.ts into main.js. It checks that the
  bundle assembles.

  Nothing here runs the game. Witnessing the harvester actually complete
  tutorial 1 against a server is the itest's job, and that harness came later —
  it's on main, and pulling it back into this baseline would be section-2-era
  tooling arriving early. For this baseline, "see it running" is you watching
  the room after deploy.

✻ Brewed for 6s

❯ walk me through how this is supposed to work, i logged onto screeps server    
  and it asked me to pick as room and then asked me to place my spawn           

● That room-pick and spawn-placement is the game's own onboarding — every
  account places its first spawn by hand, and that's the last manual act. From
  there:

  1. Pick a room and place the spawn. Put it within easy reach of an energy
  source. Keep the default name Spawn1 — the harness looks up
  Game.spawns["Spawn1"] by that exact name; a different name and the code finds
  nothing.
  2. Deploy (nix run .#deploy, if you haven't since placing). The server runs
  whatever sits on branch default every game tick — your bundled main.js is now
  the colony's only operator.
  3. First ticks: the loop sees zero creeps and a free spawn, so it calls
  spawnCreep with the spec's harvesterBody (WORK, CARRY, MOVE — 200 energy). The
  spawn accumulates energy on its own, so shortly after placement it has
  enough. You'll see harvester-<tick> emerge.
  4. The cycle you watch: free carry capacity → walk to the nearest source and
  harvest; full → walk back and transfer to Spawn1. That's tutorial 1's loop
  running from the generated decision logic plus the harness.
  5. When the creep dies (creeps age out after 1500 ticks), the memory-reclaim
  block clears its record and the spawn block builds a replacement. The colony
  keeps itself at one harvester with no console input.

  If nothing spawns after a minute or two: check the in-game console for errors,
  and check the spawn's name first — that's the single assumption the harness
  makes about the world.

✻ Worked for 21s

● That's the harvester running. When you see it reach the source, fill up, and
  walk the energy back to Spawn1, you've watched tutorial 1 complete — spec
  through Paradox, harness through tsc, bundle through the flake, running on the
  live server.

● Two ways on screeps.com:

  - Console: Game.spawns['Spawn1'].destroy() — losing your last spawn puts you
  back in room/placement mode.
  - Or the menu: the world view has a Respawn option that wipes your structures
  and returns you to placement.

  Then place the new spawn next to a source, keep the name Spawn1. Your code is
  still deployed, so once the spawn exists the loop builds a new harvester on
  its own — no redeploy needed.

❯ just give me a screenshot thing I can install on nix quick                    
● nix run nixpkgs#flameshot -- gui

  No install step — it runs straight from nixpkgs, you drag-select the region,
  and the save button writes a PNG wherever you point it. If it complains about
  Wayland, use nix run nixpkgs#grim -- shot.png instead — that captures the
  whole screen to shot.png.

  nix run nixpkgs#grim -- shot.png

  nix run nixpkgs#slurp piped in works, but for one screenshot cropping later is

✻ Cooked for 4s

❯ no, this wont work, i need something that doesnt need to be used on the       
  terminal it is run in                                                         

● A delay does that — run this, then alt-tab to the Screeps client and wait:

  sleep 5 && nix run nixpkgs#grim -- shot.png

  Five seconds after you hit enter it captures the whole screen, whatever is on
  it, into shot.png. Bump the 5 if you need more switching time.

✻ Crunched for 6s

❯ [mlitchard@polymetis:~/gitlab/dox-populi]$ sleep 5 && nix run nixpkgs#grim -- 
  shot.png

  (nix:dox-populi-dev-env)                                                      
                                                                                

● grim needs Wayland and your session doesn't have it — you're on X11. scrot is
  the X11 equivalent, with the delay built in:

  nix run nixpkgs#scrot -- -d 5 shot.png

  Five-second countdown, then full screen to shot.png.

✻ Sautéed for 6s

❯ the problem is it also captured the terminal running the capture, i'd rather  
  not have to crop                                                              

● Same command with -u — it captures only the window that has focus when the
  timer fires:

  nix run nixpkgs#scrot -- -d 5 -u shot.png

  Alt-tab to the Screeps client during the countdown and the file contains just
  that window, no terminal, no crop.

● The value is that the reader witnesses the link between the spec and the
  harness, with their own hands on it.

  The post claims decisions live in the spec. This exercise is where that claim
  stops being prose: the reader writes a harness branch for a role, and because
  the role is absent from the spec's union, tsc rejects the comparison — TS2367,
  naming both sides, pointing at the line. The reader now knows, from their own
  run, that the spec's union reaches into the harness as a type and that drift
  between the two is a compile error.

  experiences: a malformed spec gets rejected by paradox check with a located
  error; a harness that disagrees with the generated decision logic gets
  rejected by tsc with a located error. The reader leaves knowing which
  instrument catches which class of mistake and what each one's evidence looks
  like — which is exactly the skill the objective names.

  and this reader has already felt the mechanism that makes that removal safe.

✻ Brewed for 1m 35s

❯ my existing wc denial hook failed, tell me it's value                         

● It denied every command it was written to deny — this session included: my
  test payload with a bare wc in it was refused before it ran. On its own terms
  it worked.

  Its value is exactly as wide as its pattern, and its pattern was narrower than
  your law. The law is "no count or size output"; the script encoded "no wc
  command." --stat produces the same output the law bans and matched nothing, so
  the strike walked through the gap between the rule you meant and the rule the
  script checks. The hook enforced its named pattern; it never enforced the
  law.

  That's the same shape as your green-checks lesson: the instrument covers what
  it names, and what it doesn't name it says nothing about. The value of the wc
  hook is real but bounded — and today's addition widens the named patterns, it
  doesn't change that bound.

● The VM machinery is in. What came over and how:

  Verbatim from the reference: vm/disko.nix, vm/installer.nix, and run-vm.sh
  (copied byte-for-byte, with its executable bit — the run-vm-fresh check diffs
  it against the flake's render, and the hardware constants and template came
  over unchanged, isoUrl still empty pending your first release).

  Adapted, with only what this stage forced:
  - vm/module.nix — dropped SCREEPS_HOST (private-server variable) and rewrote
  the motd quickstart for this branch's apps: nix develop, nix flake check, nix 
  run .#deploy to screeps.com, with the secrix instructions now naming
  secrets/SCREEPS_TOKEN. The 9p mounts, dev user, Determinate settings, and
  direnv are unchanged.
  - tests/vm.nix — the login-environment subtest now asserts SCREEPS_IDENTITY
  only.
  - The vm image's extraDependencies bakes the devShell and all locked inputs;
  the server and client packages it listed on main don't exist here.
  - The deploy app now resolves its key as SCREEPS_IDENTITY falling back to
  ~/.ssh/gitlab — your host workflow is unchanged, and inside the VM the
  module's SCREEPS_IDENTITY=~/work/identity takes over, same pattern as main.

  Flake wiring added: disko and determinate inputs,
  nixosConfigurations.vm/installer, packages
  vm-image/vm-image-zst/installer-iso, apps gen-run-vm/installer-iso, checks
  vm-boot/run-vm-fresh, and three shellHook lines.

  Two things to know before you build: nix flake check will now pull the two new
  inputs and run run-vm-fresh, which catches any drift between the committed
  run-vm.sh and my transcription of the template — if it fails, nix run 
  .#gen-run-vm regenerates the file and the diff tells us what drifted. And nix 
  build .#installer-iso is the ISO for the Equipment link.

✻ Crunched for 7s

❯ i just did a test of the code inside the vm, intereting first observation,    
  the game would not let me place a spawn                                       

● Two candidate explanations, and which one it is depends on where you were
  placing:

  1. You were placing on screeps.com — your account still holds this morning's
  colony (the respawned harvester). The game allows one colony; the place-spawn
  flow only opens after a respawn wipes the old one. If the morning spawn is
  still alive out there, that's the refusal.
  2. You were placing against a server in the VM — this branch's VM has no
  Screeps server; the machinery I ported deliberately left out the server
  package the later branches added (the tutorial-one flake deploys to
  screeps.com only). run-vm.sh still forwards ports 21025/21026 because the
  runner script came over byte-identical, so a client pointed at localhost gets
  a dead port, and any placement attempt has nothing real behind it.

  Which path were you on? If it's the second, that's a real finding about this
  stage's reader story: the VM can build, check, and deploy, and the watching
  happens on screeps.com — the post and motd say that, but the forwarded ports
  invite the wrong expectation.

❯ ah yes I did place a colony how do I delte it>>/                              

● Same as this morning: in the game console, Game.spawns['Spawn1'].destroy() —
  losing your last spawn ends the colony and reopens placement. Or use the
  Respawn option in the world-view menu, which wipes everything at once. Then
  place fresh near a source.

✻ Sautéed for 20s

❯ how do I determine what the reasonable hardware footprint would be. Can I     
  make a config that says 'use what is available' rather than hardcoding        
  requirements? I figure this should run with 16GB RAM, 1 CPU and I have no     
  idea how much hard disk space. I also wonder to what degree I need to draw    
  attention to hardware footprint requirements. This project is something of an 
  on-ramp but if someone has inadaquate hardware, maybe I deal with that on an  
  ad-hoc basis. maybe I can assume most people have decent hardware             

● Four answers, and one fact that makes this concrete: the run-vm.sh you just
  ported defaults to 32G RAM and 8 CPUs — main's sizing, set for the private
  server holding a world database in memory. This stage's VM runs no server; the
  guest only needs to hold nix develop, the checkers, and a deploy. On the 16
  GB host you're describing, today's script fails at boot because qemu asks for
  more RAM than the machine has. So the footprint question has to be settled for
  this stage regardless.

  How to determine it: measure, on the reader path. Boot the VM at a candidate
  size, run exactly what the post asks — nix develop, nix flake check, nix run 
  .#deploy — and step the memory down until something breaks or crawls. That
  gives a floor you witnessed instead of one you modeled. Disk the same way:
  after a full reader session, the qcow2's actual allocation on the host plus
  the ISO is the real number (the 40G is virtual; qcow2 only occupies what's
  written, so the honest figure is measured, and my guess ahead of measuring is
  single-digit GB for the ISO and low tens for the disk).

  "Use what is available": yes, and it fits your ruled config pattern. run-vm.sh
  is plain bash; it can read nproc and /proc/meminfo at start and default
  MEM/CPUS to a fraction of the host — with the existing MEM=/CPUS= env vars
  staying as the overrides. Two costs: the template lives in flake.nix, so the
  change goes through gen-run-vm and the byte-match check; and it diverges this
  script from main's — a divergence main could adopt later, since auto-sizing is
  right for the server VM too.

  Your 16 GB / 1 CPU guess: plausible for this stage — the image pre-bakes the
  devShell, so the guest builds almost nothing. 1 CPU will work and feel slow on
  the first nix flake check; auto-detection makes the question moot on any
  decent machine.

  How loudly to say it: one measured line in Equipment — RAM floor and disk
  total — and nothing more. Your instinct is right for this audience: assume a
  working developer's machine, handle the exceptions when they show up. The one
  failure worth engineering away rather than documenting is the current one,
  where the default asks for more than the host has and the reader's first
  command dies; auto-sizing or a lower stage default removes it, and then the
  Equipment line is a courtesy instead of a gate.

❯ why do you think the diff check is a useful exercise, this is not a git       
  tutorial                                                                      

● Because the skill it teaches is auditing a claim, and the diff is just the
  reading tool. This post makes an assertion about its own record — the baseline
  is the earliest commit minus two lines — and it's the one post in the series
  where the reader has an actual reason to be suspicious, since the thing
  they're standing on is a reconstruction. Exercise 4 hands them the evidence
  and lets them rule on the claim themselves instead of taking your word for it.
  That's the trust doctrine performed by the reader: the diff is the evidence,
  the reader inspects, the reader decides. Every other exercise in the series
  has them verify code; this one has them verify the author.

  The audience makes the git mechanics a non-issue — a working developer runs
  diffs daily, so nothing needs teaching there, and the exercise spends its
  whole budget on the judgment call.

  That's the case. If it reads to you as git homework anyway, cutting it costs
  objectives 1–3 nothing — but then objective 4 and the Promise's "you can check
  this claim" sentence go with it, and the derivation claim stands on your
  say-so alone.

✻ Sautéed for 2m 31s

● Agent "Study the paradox repo" finished · 22m 35s

● The re-assessment, verdict first: my line undersells Paradox by describing
  only its gate. The repo's own positioning is "a language for domain
  specification" that "compiles to multiple target languages" — you define
  types, validation, and display logic once, and it generates code for seventeen
  targets from one source of truth. The checking layer exists in service of
  that: it rejects a spec that is internally contradictory before any code gets
  generated from it. So the value Paradox brings to this project is the thing
  the whole series stands on — the spec is where decisions live because Paradox 
  is the compiler that turns the spec into the decision logic the harness 
  imports. "Verifies named spec properties" describes the doorman and never
  mentions the building.

  What the checking layer actually covers, since the claim vocabulary rides on
  it: paradox check runs presence and kind checking, SMT-based type checking,
  refinement satisfiability through Z3 (its docs are careful here — an
  overconstrained type is rejected because Z3 finds the constraints
  unsatisfiable), exhaustiveness, and lemma checking, with a documented
  Limitations section. The generation side carries one policy the repo defends
  explicitly: every backend runs declared validators at runtime — "no backend
  silently degrades validation to a no-op."

  Bounds worth knowing, because they calibrate our claims:

  - Without Z3, refinement checks silently skip — a fail-open path. In our flake
  it's closed: the paradox-check derivation pins z3 into its build inputs, so
  this pipeline never runs the degraded check. That's the infrastructure earning
  its keep in a way the posts could someday use.
  - State-machine verification runs under generate, never under check —
  irrelevant to tutorial-one (no machines yet) and worth remembering from
  section 2 on.
  - The F story is layered:* generation emits real lemma obligations, but
  paradox check --fstar runs F* in lax mode, which skips discharging them; the
  repo's atlas CI runs full F*. So "generates proof obligations" is solid
  ground; where they get discharged depends on which runner.
  - The repo's internal FINDINGS.md is blunt about known unsoundness in the
  checker's inference. It's an alpha tool being honest with itself, which fits
  how the blog already treats green checks as smaller than they look.

  For line 22, the phrasing that matches the tool: Paradox rejects an 
  inconsistent spec and generates the decision logic the harness imports. The
  rejection clause keeps the named-properties discipline; the generation clause
  states the value that was missing.

※ recap: The tutorial-one off-series post and its VM/CI machinery are built: drafts written, quotes verified, flake wired, Paradox value doc added, line 22 corrected. Next action: regenerate run-vm.sh via nix run .#gen-run-vm for the auto-detect change.

❯ explain this 'the Z3 fail-open path'                                          

● Paradox doesn't contain Z3 — it calls a separate z3 executable it expects to
  find on the PATH. The fail-open is what happens when that executable is
  missing: paradox check still runs, still exits green, and simply skips every
  check that needed the solver — refinement satisfiability, division-by-zero —
  with no warning printed. In the source, the "Z3 solver not available" result
  is explicitly mapped to "do nothing and continue."

  "Fail-open" is the security term for that shape: when the checking machinery
  is unavailable, the gate stays open instead of the run failing. The
  consequence is that two green paradox check runs can mean different things —
  on a machine with z3, green includes the solver-backed checks; on a machine
  without it, green covers only parsing, presence, and kinds, and looks
  identical.

  Our flake closes the path: the paradox-check derivation lists z3 in its build
  inputs, so in this pipeline the check cannot run without the solver present.
  Every green from nix flake check is the full-coverage green.

  dont see this in my flake

● The evidence, from your flake.nix as it stands right now:

  - flake.nix:434-436 — the paradox-check check: nativeBuildInputs = [ 
  paradoxBin pkgs.z3 ];
  - flake.nix:54 — the generated derivation carries the same pair, so generation
  runs with the solver present too.
  - flake.nix:406 — pkgs.z3 in the devShell packages, which is why paradox check
  run by hand inside nix develop also has it.

  The z3 sits in the same bracket as paradoxBin on those lines, which may be why
  it didn't jump out — it's an element of the nativeBuildInputs list, two
  identifiers apart from the tool it serves.

❯ is the dox-populi directory in the vm mounted from host?                      

● Yes. Two pieces make it so: run-vm.sh shares the directory the script lives in
  — the host checkout — into qemu as the 9p tag repodir ("Shares the repo
  read-write into the guest"), and vm/module.nix mounts that tag at
  /home/dev/dox-populi (9p, rw, nofail). So the guest's ~/dox-populi is the host
  repo, live in both directions — an edit on either side is immediately visible
  on the other. The nofail option means the VM still boots when the share is
  absent, such as a reader running the ISO's disk image without the repo beside
  the script.

✻ Cooked for 9s

❯ I have added 22-24 and now want a description of the development workflow as  
  you see it                                                                    

  Read 1 file (ctrl+o to expand)

● The workflow as I see it, from the machinery on this branch:

  Getting in. On the host: check out tutorial-one, put the stage ISO beside
  run-vm.sh, run ./run-vm.sh. The first run creates the disk and boots the
  installer once — it writes the prebuilt system image and powers off; every run
  after boots the installed VM in a tmux session. The host needs qemu, tmux,
  and curl. Inside, you're auto-logged-in as dev, and ~/dox-populi is the host
  checkout mounted live over 9p — so you can edit with your host editor and run
  tools in the guest against the same files.

  The environment. nix develop in ~/dox-populi drops into the devShell,
  pre-baked into the VM image so it opens without fetching anything. That shell
  carries Paradox, Z3, tsc, esbuild, and the secrix command.

  The loop. Two files hold the program: dox/creeps.dox (the spec) and
  harness/main.ts (the harness). Edit either, then nix flake check. The check
  rebuilds everything from the spec each time: Paradox checks the spec and
  generates the decision logic in-derivation, tsc typechecks the harness against
  that freshly generated logic, esbuild assembles main.js. Because generation
  happens inside the build, the harness always compiles against the current spec
  — there is no stale-generated-code state to be in. A spec edit that breaks
  the harness surfaces as a compile error on this run, at a line.

  Delivery. nix run .#deploy rebuilds main.js from whatever the spec and harness
  now say, decrypts your Screeps token (the VM points SCREEPS_IDENTITY at
  ~/work/identity, the key you shared in from the host via the workdir), and
  pushes to your account's default branch on screeps.com. The server runs the
  new code on its next tick. You watch the result in the room — for this branch,
  one harvester spawning itself, harvesting, and delivering.

  The shape of it: edit → check → deploy → watch, with the checks between you
  and the server on every pass, and the same nix flake check a reader runs being
  the one CI runs.

❯ Due to the accessibility requirement, we must remove the requirement to       
  purchase Screeps. We must include the server/client code from dox-main into   
  this branch. make a plan and show me the plan to do this                      

● Here's the plan. Two things in it are open questions the scout step must
  answer before the wiring is trustworthy, and one is a page-level consequence
  you'll want to rule on.

  1. Scout dox-main (read only).
  - The server chain: the screepsServer derivation (npm-vendored open-source
  server, the isolated-vm git dependency, the pinned npmDepsHash), the server/
  directory (package.json, mods.json), and tickMs.
  - The client chain: the client/ directory and screepsClient derivation —
  specifically where the game assets come from. Main's run-vm.sh links
  package.nw out of the host's Steam install of Screeps. If that's the only
  asset path, the purchase requirement hasn't died; it's moved. The scout
  determines whether the client can serve without a purchased copy, because
  that's the whole point of this change.
  - The apps: server, deploy-local (self-provisioning: creates the account,
  pushes main.js, places Spawn1), cli, client, stop, reset-local, and what
  deploy-local needs from secrets (SCREEPS_LOCAL_CREDS, with env-var override).
  - What in mods.json belongs to later eras — the raid mod and invader machinery
  are attack-defense work and stay out; the scout confirms the server runs
  clean without them for this stage.

  2. Copy and wire (the reuse step).
  - server/ and client/ directories in, verbatim where era-appropriate.
  - Flake: the two derivations plus tickMs, packages
  screeps-server/screeps-client, the six apps, shellHook lines.
  - VM: restore SCREEPS_HOST=0.0.0.0 to vm/module.nix (I removed it when
  porting, correctly for a serverless stage — the server brings it back), add
  the two packages to the VM image's extraDependencies exactly as main does, and
  return the motd quickstart to the server workflow (server → deploy-local →
  client). The 21025/21026/8080 port forwards already sit in run-vm.sh unchanged
  from main, so the runner needs nothing.
  - The screeps.com deploy app stays as an option; deploy-local becomes the
  reader path.

  3. Known risk, stated up front. npmDepsHash and the isolated-vm build were
  pinned against main's flake.lock. This branch's lock resolves independently,
  so the server build may fail at the hash or at node-gyp — the failure mode is
  loud and the fix is re-pinning, but it's the step most likely to need
  iteration. You'll likely want to run nix build .#screeps-server yourself once
  the wiring lands.

  4. Consequences on the page (your rulings, listed for when the machinery 
  works):
  - "What delivery looks like" and the postscript's deploy exercise move from
  screeps.com to deploy-local + the browser client on :8080.
  - Equipment loses the Screeps-account/token requirement.
  - The Session transcript witnesses a screeps.com deploy — the record stays as
  it happened while the branch's reader path becomes the local server. That
  mismatch needs one sentence somewhere, in your words.

  Out of scope unless you say otherwise: the itest (your earlier ruling — this
  branch's checks stay paradox-check/typecheck/build/vm-boot/run-vm-fresh), the
  invader/raid machinery, and the NPC user.

  Say go and I start with the scout.

※ recap: We're building the off-series tutorial-one blog post and its branch, now planning to bring the private Screeps server and client over from dox-main so readers don't buy Screeps. Awaiting your go on the plan's scout step.

❯ this is what gemini says about thast 'es, it is entirely possible to run your 
  own Screeps server without purchasing the game.Because the backend core of    
  the Screeps game engine is completely open source, you can host it for free   
  on your own machine, a local network, or a dedicated cloud server (like AWS   
  or DigitalOcean).However, doing so comes with a major catch regarding how you 
  interact with it:1. The Server is Free and Open SourceYou can download,       
  install, and run the server headless via Node.js using tools like the         
  community-maintained screeps-launcher. It supports community mods and custom  
  configurations.2. The Official Client is PaidWhile the backend server is      
  free, the official Screeps graphical user interface (the client) requires you 
  to buy the game on Steam. If you try to connect to a server using the         
  official client, it looks for Steam authentication to verify that you own the 
  game. How to play on your free server for freeIf you do not want to purchase  
  the game on Steam, you cannot use the official desktop client. Instead, you   
  must use community-driven workarounds to interact with your                   
  server:Alternative Clients: Since the communication protocol between the      
  client and the server is open, the community has built alternative            
  open-source clients and IDE plugins (such as Screep Studio or custom web      
  interfaces) that allow you to connect without Steam auth.Authentication Mods: 
  To bypass the Steam login requirement on your server, you will need to        
  install a server modification such as screepsmod-auth. This allows you and    
  your friends to register and log in using a standard username and password    
  instead of a Steam token.CLI Control: You can technically push code and       
  interact with the server programmatically through the Command Line Interface  
  (CLI) and API, though you won't have a visual representation of your creeps   
  moving around the map'                                                        

● Gemini's picture matches the risk the plan already carries, and it sharpens
  the fork. The server half is settled ground — main's server is the open-source
  engine, nix-vendored, and its deploy-local flow already does
  username/password provisioning, so the auth-mod concern is solved territory.
  The whole question lives in the client half: main's browser client serves the
  game out of package.nw, and run-vm.sh links that file from the host's Steam
  install. If that's the only asset source, the purchase hasn't been removed —
  it's been relocated to "whoever supplies package.nw," and a reader without the
  game gets a running server they can only reach through the CLI, with no
  creeps to watch. The watching is what the series sells, so that outcome fails
  your accessibility requirement even with everything built.

  So the scout step decides the plan's shape: it reads main's client/ to
  establish what the steamless client actually needs, whether the assets can be
  obtained without a purchase, and what an alternative client would cost us if
  they can't. Everything downstream — which apps come over, what the motd says,
  what "What delivery looks like" becomes — hangs on that answer.

  Say go and I'll run the scout with that as its lead question.

● The lead question is answered, and it's the bad branch of the fork: as wired 
  on main, watching requires the purchase. The evidence, from main's flake:

  - Line 912: the client app's own comment — "serve the official client assets
  from the local Steam install of the game (package.nw — you must own Screeps)",
  and its error message tells the user to "install the Screeps game via Steam"
  (line 936).
  - The screepsClient derivation comment (407–408): the assets are "proprietary"
  and come from the Steam install; the steamless client is a bridge that serves
  them, with the purchase relocated exactly as we suspected.
  - The server half is genuinely free: open-source screeps 4.3.0 launcher,
  screepsmod-auth for password login, and the server's own assets symlink points
  into the launcher's init_dist/assets (line 775) — free, though what that
  directory holds (full client, or just map tiles and badges) I can't see from
  here, since it lives inside the npm package nix fetches at build.
  - One more free-side fact: screepsrc wants a Steam Web API key, which needs
  only a free Steam account, no game purchase — and password auth via the mod
  may sidestep even that for readers.

  So meeting your accessibility requirement needs one thing main doesn't have: a
  viewer that renders the world without the proprietary package.nw. Two places
  an answer could live:

  1. The launcher's own init_dist/assets — if the open-source server ships
  enough client to serve, the whole problem dissolves with what we already
  vendor. This is where your download offer lands: fetch the open-source server
  (the screeps npm package or its GitHub repo) somewhere I can read, and I'll
  tell you what's actually in there.
  2. The community viewer landscape — the official renderer was open-sourced as
  @screeps/renderer, and viewers may exist built on it with free assets. That's
  web research, off your machine.

  My recommendation: do 1 first — it's your own dependency, already trusted, and
  the cheapest possible answer if it's yes. Say which you want.

✻ Cogitated for 30s

❯ doesn't dox-main use an open source client?                                   

● The tool is open source; what it serves is the paid part.
  screeps-steamless-client — the npm package main vendors — is an open-source
  bridge: it proxies your browser to the private server and serves the game's
  UI. But the UI it serves is the official client itself, package.nw, which it
  reads off a Steam install of the game. Main's own comments state both halves:
  the client package.json calls it "screeps-steamless-client, nix-vendored —
  serves the official (Steam-installed) client assets to a web browser," and the
  client app's comment in the flake says the assets come "from a Steam install
  of the GAME — proprietary," with the app erroring out with "you must own
  Screeps" when it can't find package.nw.

  So dox-main removed Steam-the-launcher from the loop and kept
  Steam-the-purchase: an open-source pipe carrying proprietary water. The viewer
  question stands — what's missing for your accessibility requirement is an
  open-source renderer with its own assets, and that's the web research.

❯ you are wasting too much , here is what I found 'Option 1: Screeps Standalone 
  Client (Recommended)The screepers/screeps-standalone-client is a community    
  project that extracts the official web client assets so you can host them     
  yourself. It runs as a web server on your machine, allowing anyone to access  
  the full graphical game interface via a standard web browser without needing  
  Steam.How it works: It acts as a bridge. You host the client files on your    
  server (e.g., on port 8080), open your browser, and point the UI to your      
  Screeps server's API port (usually 21025).Deployment: It is easiest to deploy 
  via Docker. You can pull the official image and run it with a single command  
  alongside your server. Option 2: Screeps Steamless Client (Proxy Method)If    
  you want the client files embedded directly into your server package,         
  screepers/steamless-client is a node package that injects a web client        
  directly into your local private server instance.How it works: Once           
  installed, visiting your server's IP address directly in a browser serves the 
  entire game GUI automatically.Requirement: This package requires a one-time   
  utility to download the client package from the public Screeps CDN to your    
  server.'                                                                      
● That closes the fork, and it's the best outcome: the fix is a mode change to
  the client path we already vendor. The assets come from the public Screeps CDN
  at the reader's machine — no Steam, no purchase, and the no-redistribution
  constraint stays satisfied because the ISO ships the fetcher, never the
  assets. First nix run .#client downloads the client package once into the work
  dir; after that it serves exactly as main's does.

  Revised plan, client half only — the server half stands as scouted:

  1. Port the server chain from main as planned — vendored screeps 4.3.0,
  screepsmod-auth, deploy-local, cli, stop, reset-local, tickMs, the screepsrc
  template, VM env restored.
  2. Client: keep the vendored bridge, replace the package.nw hunt with the CDN
  fetch. Main pins screeps-steamless-client 1.2.1 — whether that version carries
  the CDN downloader or needs a bump (or the screeps-standalone-client package
  instead) gets settled by reading the pinned package's docs when I wire it; the
  lockfile changes either way, so the npm hash gets re-pinned in the same
  motion.
  3. The client app's fallback chain changes: work-dir cached copy first, CDN
  fetch if absent — the Steam-install path and its "you must own Screeps" error
  go away entirely on this branch.
  4. Downstream page consequences as listed before: delivery section, equipment,
  motd — all after the machinery works.

  Say go and I start the port.

      737 +                echo "place YOUR key there, or set SCREEPS_IDE
          +NTITY=/path/to/your/key" >&2                                  
      738 +                exit 1                                        
      739 +              fi                                              
      740 +              CREDS=$(${secrixCli}/bin/secrix decrypt secrets/
          +SCREEPS_LOCAL_CREDS -i "$IDENTITY")                           
      741 +              SCREEPS_LOCAL_EMAIL="''${SCREEPS_LOCAL_EMAIL:-''
          +${CREDS%%:*}}"                                                
      742 +              SCREEPS_LOCAL_PASSWORD="''${SCREEPS_LOCAL_PASSWO
          +RD:-''${CREDS#*:}}"                                           
      743 +            fi                                                
      744 +            : "''${SCREEPS_LOCAL_EMAIL:?set SCREEPS_LOCAL_EMAI
          +L or create secrets/SCREEPS_LOCAL_CREDS}"                     
      745 +            : "''${SCREEPS_LOCAL_PASSWORD:?set SCREEPS_LOCAL_P
          +ASSWORD or create secrets/SCREEPS_LOCAL_CREDS}"               
      746 +

      750 +            CLI_PORT="''${SCREEPS_LOCAL_CLI_PORT:-21026}"     
      751 +                                                              
      752 +            signin() {

          +\

          +" --arg p "$SCREEPS_LOCAL_PASSWORD" \

          + >&2

      784 +              '{branch: "default", modules: {main: $code}}' \

          +r/code" \

          +tion/json" --data @-                                          
      787 +            echo

      789 +                                                              
      790 +            # Auto-spawn: if the account owns nothing yet, pla
          +ce Spawn1.                                                    
      791 +            # place-spawn demands a room that exists in db.roo
          +ms AND has                                                    
      792 +            # an unowned controller (the server's world-start-
          +room falls                                                    
      793 +            # back to W5N5, a controller-less center room). No
          + HTTP                                                         
      794 +            # endpoint exposes controller ownership, so read c
          +andidates

      796 +            STATUS=$($CURL -sS -H "X-Token: $TOKEN" "$URL/api/
          +user/world-status" \

      800 +              # Owns objects but no spawn+controller pair (spa
          +wn destroyed,

          + releases the

          +/user/respawn" >/dev/null

          +i/user/world-status" \

          +git rev-parse --show-toplevel)/.server-data}"

          +name == "rooms.objects")

      817 +                           and ((.reservation // null) == null
          +))

          +n?room=$ROOM&encoded=true" \

          +GIN {

          + -1; k -= 2) {                                                
      826 +                    i = 1275 + d * k

          +int(i / 50)

          +ff the room edges

          + < 47 && y > 2 && y < 47) {                                   
      831 +                      print i; exit                           
      832 +                    }                                         
      833 +                  }                                           
      834 +                }')                                           
      835 +                [ -z "$IDX" ] && continue                     
      836 +                X=$((IDX % 50)); Y=$((IDX / 50))              
      837 +                RESULT=$($CURL -sS -X POST "$URL/api/game/plac
          +e-spawn" \

          +cation/json" \

      841 +                if [ "$(printf '%s' "$RESULT" | $JQ -r '.ok //

● The server chain is wired. Where things stand:

  In place:
  - server/ and client/ vendoring directories copied from main (package.json +
  lockfile + npm-deps-hash each).
  - Flake: serverNode/screepsServer/serverMods derivations, tickMs = 1000
  (changed from main's 50 — this world is for watching, and the engine default
  is the official pace; SCREEPS_TICK_MS overrides), apps server, cli,
  deploy-local, stop, reset-local, and the three lock-* pinning apps. The
  screeps-server package is exported and baked into the VM image.
  - De-raiding: the server app injects only screepsmod-auth into mods.json, the
  world seeds from the plain launcher database (the raiders NPC and invader stub
  stayed on main), and deploy-local keeps the newbie protection that
  place-spawn welds on — main cleared it to invite raids; this stage wants the
  protection.
  - No Steam anywhere in the reader path: the server starts without a Steam Web
  API key and says so; signin is username/password through the auth mod;
  deploy-local self-provisions.
  - VM: SCREEPS_HOST=0.0.0.0 restored, motd quickstart now runs server →
  deploy-local with the SCREEPS_LOCAL_CREDS instructions.

  Not yet in: the client app. The steamless bridge is vendored but wiring it
  would reintroduce "you must own Screeps," so the viewer slot waits for
  keeshii's client — MIT, own assets. The open vendoring question: whether its
  npm artifact ships a built app or we build from the GitHub repo with
  buildNpmPackage (its repo has webpack + a lockfile to check). That's the next
  work item.

  Your verification steps when ready: nix build .#screeps-server first — it's
  the npmDepsHash/isolated-vm risk spot flagged in the plan — then nix run 
  .#server and nix run .#deploy-local inside the VM or on the host.

✻ Cooked for 5m 51s

❯ it must all work in the vm or it doesn't work                                 

● Agreed, and that bar shapes the viewer decision in a good way. The server half
  already meets it: the vendored server is baked into the VM image's
  extraDependencies, the world seeds from the launcher's own files, the guest
  fetches nothing, and it binds 0.0.0.0 so the ports run-vm.sh already forwards
  (21025/21026/8080) reach the host. The reader's browser lives on the host and
  looks through those forwards — the VM stays headless.

  For the viewer it means one thing decisively: no CDN fetch at reader time.
  Keeshii's client gets built by nix and baked into the ISO like everything else
  — which its MIT license permits and the proprietary package.nw never could.
  The guest serves it on 8080, offline, and the host browser watches. That's the
  standard the client work will be built and judged against: boot the VM with
  no network assumptions beyond the forwards, run server → deploy-local →
  client, see the harvester from the host browser.

✻ Sautéed for 18s

※ recap: Goal: make tutorial-one fully playable in the VM without buying Screeps. The private server chain is wired into the flake; next is vendoring keeshii's MIT client as the baked-in viewer, then you build and test in the VM.

  ⎿  Interrupted · What should Claude do instead?

❯ stop this is an incomplete client not usable, what are the alternatives?      

● Stopped. The honest picture of the alternatives, from everything the research
  has turned up:

  1. Build a minimal viewer on the official open renderer. Screeps open-sourced
  @screeps/renderer with the same code and images the real client uses — that's
  the one complete, legal, free rendering engine in existence. What doesn't
  exist is the glue: a page that signs in, subscribes to the room's socket feed,
  and hands the object stream to the renderer. That glue is the buildable piece
  — display-only, no editor, no mouse, which is exactly the reader path's shape
  since deploy-local already does everything interactive. Real work, but
  bounded, and it's the only option that produces a complete, ours-to-keep
  viewer.
  2. Screep Studio — an open-source native Qt client for private servers, from
  the community. Age and maintenance state unknown to me; it predates current
  server versions. Cheap to evaluate, and if it works it's a host-side
  application the reader runs pointed at the forwarded ports — nothing baked in
  the ISO. Evaluation cost is small; the risk is bit-rot.
  3. The steamless bridge stays, purchase becomes optional-but-visual. The wired
  server path works for everyone; watching in full fidelity remains a perk for
  game owners. This fails your stated requirement as the reader path — listed
  because it's what dox-main chose, and it may survive as the fallback tier
  alongside whichever option wins.
  4. Recorded proof instead of live watching — a headless renderer (the same
  open renderer, screeps-cap-style) producing GIFs/video from the reader's own
  world. Automatic and legal, but the reader watches a recording of their world
  rather than their world.

  My recommendation is 1, with 2 evaluated first since it costs one look: if
  Screep Studio runs against server 4.3.0, the problem is solved without
  building anything; if it doesn't, the renderer-glue viewer is the path that
  can't be taken away from you.

  One housekeeping fact either way: the keeshii wiring I'd started (the viewer
  derivation, plus screepsmod-cors in the mods) — the cors mod and the
  static-serve pattern carry over unchanged to any web viewer including option
  1, so nothing there is wasted yet; the keeshii-specific derivation gets
  replaced by whichever candidate wins. Which way do you want to go?

✻ Crunched for 43s

❯ what is the url for screep studio                                             

● Web Search("Screep Studio GitHub Qt client screeps private server")
  ⎿  Did 1 search in 7s

● MrShecks/screep-studio (https://github.com/MrShecks/screep-studio) — "a native
  cross platform client for the Screeps MMO sandbox game," C++/Qt, from the
  Level1Techs Devember 2018 event.

  Two facts from the search worth having before you look: it connects to private
  servers, but "there is currently no UI for selecting a server, logging on or
  opening rooms, as this is hard-coded in ScreepStudioApplication.cpp" — which
  cuts both ways, since we'd be nix-building it anyway and could pin the
  hardcoded target to localhost:21025. And it's a 2018 project, so the bit-rot
  risk against server 4.3.0 is real. Sources: screep-studio
  (https://github.com/MrShecks/screep-studio), screeps-launcher
  (https://github.com/screepers/screeps-launcher), screeps/screeps
  (https://github.com/screeps/screeps).

✻ Cooked for 19s

❯ forget it. we will build 1. but we need to be able to use the mouse to        
  interact with objects. first assess whatever work you have done that is no    
  longer needed. then come up with a plan to implement the typescript client    

● No-longer-needed assessment:

  - The keeshii viewer derivation in flake.nix (the screepsViewer block pinning
  f150e9b with the two fake hashes) — dead. It gets replaced by a derivation
  that builds our own source; nothing in it survives except the shape
  (buildNpmPackage → serve static output), which the new plan reuses.
  - The screepsmod-cors addition to server/mods — still needed. Any browser
  viewer served on 8080 has to reach the API on 21025 across origins; that's
  viewer-independent. Keeps its place, still wants your nix run .#lock-mods
  re-pin.
  - The client/ steamless vendoring copied from main — no longer serves this
  branch's goal; it exists to serve purchased assets. It's unwired (I never
  ported the old client app), so it's inert. Deleting it and the lock-client app
  is your call; nothing depends on either.
  - Everything else — server chain, apps, VM wiring, cors — stays as the
  foundation the viewer sits on.

  The plan: a TypeScript viewer in this repo, built on @screeps/renderer.

  1. Research step (one agent, read-only): the renderer's contract. The
  screeps/renderer repo ships samples showing how to initialize it — what
  metadata pack it wants (@screeps/renderer-metadata or in-repo), what
  object-state format it consumes, how terrain is supplied, and its license
  text. Same pass establishes the socket protocol details from the
  community-documented API (signin → token, /api/game/room-terrain, websocket
  subscribe room:<name>, diff format). Everything downstream hangs on this
  contract, so it goes first.
  2. viewer/ in this repo, vendored exactly like server/mods: package.json +
  committed lockfile + npm-deps-hash, a lock-viewer app for re-pinning,
  TypeScript source, webpack (or esbuild, which we already use) building to
  dist/. Written by the agent, checked by tsc — it joins the same story as the
  harness.
  3. Four source modules:
    - Connection: signin form → token (screepsmod-auth's endpoint, same creds as
  deploy-local), room list from the API.
    - Room feed: websocket subscribe, maintain the room's object map by applying
  diffs per tick.
    - Render: hand terrain + object state to @screeps/renderer each tick — its
  code and images are the official ones, open.
    - Mouse interaction (your requirement): click → convert canvas coordinates
  to the 50×50 tile grid → look up objects at that tile in our own object map →
  side panel showing the object's live fields (type, hits, store, owner); hover
  highlight the same way. Picking against our own map instead of renderer
  internals keeps us independent of its undocumented surface.
  4. Wiring (the pattern already built this session): screepsViewer derivation
  over ./viewer, apps.client serving dist/ on 8080, package export, baked into
  the VM image's extraDependencies, motd step, shellHook line.
  5. Acceptance, per your bar: in the VM — server, deploy-local, client; from
  the host browser: sign in, watch the harvester cycle, click it, read its
  fields. Nothing fetched at reader time, nothing proprietary on the ISO.

  Scope for this stage stops at room view + object inspection. Console pane,
  code editing, and map view are later-stage candidates if ever.

  Say go and I start with the research step.

✻ Crunched for 48s

❯ and this is the only option to avoid screeps purchase?                        

● Among everything verified, yes — with one honest hedge.

  The field as established: the official client is the purchase, and both
  steamless bridges just relocate it. The two open web clients are unusable —
  keeshii's has no mouse interaction by its own feature list, Josef37's says
  "you can't really use the client for now." Screep Studio is a 2018 project
  with its connection hardcoded in source. The asset-download story Gemini
  offered didn't survive checking the actual READMEs — and even if a CDN fetch
  existed, the assets stay proprietary, so they could never go on your ISO.
  Recordings fail your mouse requirement by construction. What remains is the
  one complete, open, legal foundation: the renderer Screeps themselves
  open-sourced, plus glue we write.

  The hedge: my sweep hit the known names, and it wasn't exhaustive — GitHub's
  screeps-client topic page exists and I haven't walked all of it. If you want
  certainty before committing to a build, one agent walking that topic and the
  screepers org with "renders rooms + mouse + no package.nw + alive" as the bar
  would close the question for good. My expectation is it finds forks of the
  same dead projects — the field is small and the official-assets gravity is
  strong — but expectation is what I had about the CDN story too, and checking
  beat expecting.

  So: the build is the only option currently on the table that meets all three
  of your constraints (no purchase, mouse interaction, works in the VM). Run the
  exhaustive sweep first, or start the build's research step?

● Updated plan
  ⎿  /plan to preview
● User approved Claude's plan
  ⎿  Plan saved to: ~/.claude/plans/pure-mapping-aho.md · /plan to edit
     TypeScript viewer for the private Screeps server

     Context

     The tutorial-one branch must let a reader run the whole loop — build,
     deploy, watch — inside the VM with no Screeps purchase. The server half
     is wired (nix-vendored screeps 4.3.0, screepsmod-auth,
     deploy-local self-provisioning). The watching half has no usable
     open client: an exhaustive sweep (GitHub topics, screepers org,
     official third-party list) found every browser client dead or
     mouse-less, both steamless bridges purchase-bound, and the one
     complete client (Screeps3D) a host-side unlicensed Unity app —
     rejected by ruling to keep "host needs only QEMU." Decision: build our
     own viewer on @screeps/renderer, the officially open-sourced
     renderer + images ("custom graphics for private servers, and
     third-party GUI utilities" — blog.screeps.com/2018/08/renderer).
     Nothing in the ecosystem consumes it yet; the renderer repo's samples
     are the contract reference. Requirements: render the room live, mouse
     interaction (hover highlight, click → object detail panel), MIT-clean,
     built by nix, baked into the ISO, served from the VM on 8080 to the
     host browser.

     Step 0 — remove superseded wiring

     - flake.nix: delete the keeshii screepsViewer derivation (the
     fetchFromGitHub f150e9b... block with two fakeHashes).
     - Keep: screepsmod-cors in server/mods/package.json (any web viewer
     on 8080 needs it; Michael reruns nix run .#lock-mods to pin).
     - client/ (steamless vendoring) and lock-client app: inert;
     deletion is Michael's call, not part of this plan.

     Step 1 — renderer contract research (read-only agent)

     Read github.com/screeps/renderer (and its samples/ + package.json +
     LICENSE) to establish: init API (world/resource config), the metadata
     pack it needs (@screeps/renderer-metadata or in-repo), the object
     state shape it consumes per tick, terrain input, and its license text
     verbatim. Also pin the server socket protocol details against
     python-screeps / node-screeps-api docs: signin → token, websocket
     endpoint (/socket/websocket), auth frame, subscribe room:<name>,
     the room diff format (full state first frame, per-tick diffs after).
     Output: a contract note at docs/viewer-contract.md.

     Step 2 — viewer/ package (vendored like server/mods)

     - viewer/package.json — deps: @screeps/renderer (+ metadata pack
     per step 1). Dev deps: typescript, esbuild (matches the repo's
     toolchain; no webpack).
     - Committed package-lock.json + npm-deps-hash (pinned by a new
     lock-viewer app, same shape as lock-mods,
     flake.nix:apps lock-mods is the pattern).
     - viewer/src/ TypeScript, tsc-checked by a new flake check
     (viewer-typecheck, mirrors the existing typecheck derivation).

     Step 3 — source modules (viewer/src/)

     1. connection.ts — signin form → /api/auth/signin token; room
     discovery via /api/user/rooms (fallback: room name input).
     2. feed.ts — websocket client: auth frame, subscribe room:<name>, maintain
     object map by applying per-tick diffs;
     expose tick stream.
     3. render.ts — init @screeps/renderer with terrain
     (/api/game/room-terrain) + metadata; push object state per tick.
     4. inspect.ts — mouse layer: canvas coords → 50×50 tile → objects
     at tile from our own object map (independent of renderer
     internals); hover outline overlay; click → side panel with live
     fields (type, hits, store, owner, name).
     5. index.ts + index.html — shell: connect form, room view, panel.

     Step 4 — flake wiring (patterns all exist in flake.nix)

     - screepsViewer derivation: buildNpmPackage over ./viewer,
     npmDepsHash from viewer/npm-deps-hash, esbuild bundle to
     dist/, install dist/ as the servable root.
     - apps.client: serve ${screepsViewer} on 8080
     (python3 -m http.server, bind SCREEPS_CLIENT_HOST defaulting to
     SCREEPS_HOST — same knob chain as the server app).
     - packages.screeps-viewer; VM extraDependencies += viewer;
     shellHook line; vm/module.nix motd step 6 ("nix run .#client —
     watch at http://localhost:8080") and a Play line for the host
     browser.
     - run-vm.sh already forwards 8080 — no runner change.

     Verification (Michael runs; the bar is "works in the VM")

     1. nix run .#lock-mods then nix build .#screeps-server (hash
     re-pin from the cors addition).
     2. nix run .#lock-viewer then nix build .#screeps-viewer.
     3. In the VM: nix run .#server, nix run .#deploy-local,
     nix run .#client.
     4. Host browser → localhost:8080: sign in with deploy-local creds,
     watch the harvester cycle, hover it (outline), click it (panel
     shows creep fields live).
     5. nix flake check — viewer-typecheck alongside the existing checks.

     Out of scope

     Console pane, code editor, map view, multi-room; itest; blog-page
     updates (delivery section, equipment, motd wording rulings) — those
     follow once the machinery is witnessed working.

● The viewer is built and wired. What's on disk:

  docs/viewer-contract.md — the research findings the implementation stands on:
  renderer init sequence, the full-snapshot-per-tick applyState contract,
  terrain format, token rotation, socket framing, snapshot-then-diffs feed, and
  the private-server quirks (no shards, no users map in the feed).

  viewer/ — the TypeScript client:
  - protocol.ts — REST with the rotating-token cell, terrain sniffing (encoded
  fallback), room discovery, room-objects seeding.
  - feed.ts — websocket: text auth frame, [channel, payload] parsing, defensive
  gz: inflation, resubscribe-on-auth.
  - state.ts — the accumulated object map: snapshot seed, diff merge (including
  the index-keyed-diff-onto-array trap the official demo handles), user
  resolution, tile lookup.
  - render.ts — renderer wrapper with the demo's proven worldConfigs, vendored
  resourceMap pointing at dist/assets/, and a fixed whole-room camera so
  pixel→tile mapping stays exact.
  - inspect.ts — your mouse requirement: hover highlight on the tile grid, click
  → side panel with live fields (owner, hits, energy, store, body, level,
  progress), re-rendered every tick.
  - main.ts + index.html — connect form (server defaults to the page's host
  on 21025), status line, room + panel layout.

  Flake: screepsViewer derivation (build bundles with esbuild and copies the
  metadata package's images — the page serves fully offline), viewer-typecheck
  check, apps.client serving on 8080, lock-viewer pinning app, package export,
  baked into the VM image, motd step 6 with the host-browser watch line.

  Your verification sequence:
  1. nix run .#lock-mods (cors addition) and nix run .#lock-viewer (first pin —
  replaces the placeholder hash).
  2. nix build .#screeps-server and nix build .#screeps-viewer.
  3. In the VM: server → deploy-local → client; host browser at localhost:8080.

  Two places most likely to need iteration at witness time, flagged honestly:
  the renderer's cameraPosition/zoomLevel units are undocumented (the
  fixed-camera math is my reading of CELL_SIZE-based world units), and the first
  nix build .#screeps-viewer is the first compile of this code against the real
  packages — viewer-typecheck checks my code against my own declarations, and
  the declarations are only as good as the contract research.

● The viewer build needs its lockfile first — viewer/package-lock.json doesn't
  exist yet, and that's what the "No lock file!" is. The lock-viewer step
  generates it and replaces the placeholder hash:

  nix run .#lock-viewer

  Then rerun nix run .#installer-iso. The ISO pulls the viewer through the VM
  image's extraDependencies, so the pin has to exist before any ISO or viewer
  build. Two things in your output are expected: the lock-mods "missing
  'resolved' URLs" warnings are the known npm-cli quirk the message links
  (main's mods pin shows the same), and the first viewer build after the pin may
  still fail once with a hash mismatch that prints the real npm-deps-hash — if
  it does, that printed value replaces the file's contents and the next build
  proceeds.

✻ Brewed for 14s

❯ I forgot my username and password                                             

● Three ways back in, shortest first:

  1. If you stored them with secrix: secrix decrypt secrets/SCREEPS_LOCAL_CREDS 
  -i "$SCREEPS_IDENTITY" (or -i <path to your key>) prints the username:password
  line.
  2. If you only ever set env vars: pick a new password and rerun deploy-local
  with the same username — it self-heals. On signin failure it pushes the
  password to the server through the CLI (auth.setPassword) and retries, so
  SCREEPS_LOCAL_EMAIL=<username> SCREEPS_LOCAL_PASSWORD=<newpass> nix run 
  .#deploy-local resets the account's password as a side effect.
  3. If the username is gone too: the server CLI knows it — nix run .#cli, then
  storage.db.users.find() and look at the username fields (your account is the
  one that isn't a system user). Then route 2 with that name.

✻ Crunched for 1m 1s

❯ i tried to log in got failed to fetch error                                   

● "Failed to fetch" is the browser refusing the cross-origin call — the page on
  8080 calling the server on 21025. Two causes fit, and the browser console
  tells you which:

  1. The running server predates the cors mod. screepsmod-cors only loads at
  server launch — the server app rewrites the world's mods.json each start. If
  the server process you're talking to was started before the lock-mods re-pin
  (or hasn't been restarted since), the mod isn't in it and every fetch from the
  viewer's origin dies exactly like this. Fix: nix run .#stop, then nix run 
  .#server again, then retry the login.
  2. The server isn't reachable at all from where the browser sits. Probe from
  the same machine as the browser: curl http://127.0.0.1:21025/api/version — a
  JSON answer means reachable, connection-refused means the port forward or
  server is the problem.

  Open the browser devtools console (F12) for the failed attempt: a line saying
  "blocked by CORS policy" confirms cause 1; ERR_CONNECTION_REFUSED confirms
  cause 2. If it's CORS and the restart alone doesn't clear it, the mod may need
  an origin setting — tell me what the console says and I'll take it from
  there.

❯ socket not connected

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● The probe answers it: both 21025 and 8080 give "connection reset" from the
  host — qemu accepts the forwarded connection and the guest sends it away,
  which means the VM is up but nothing is listening on either port inside it.
  The server and the viewer processes aren't running in the guest right now;
  "socket not connected" is downstream of that.

  So: in the VM, start nix run .#server and watch what it prints (its log goes
  to the console). The thing to look for is whether it comes up clean or dies
  during startup — the cors mod is new since your last working run, and a mod
  failing to load would show right there in the launch output. Once the server
  holds, nix run .#client in a second console, then the login. If the server
  does die on launch, paste its output and that's the real error to chase.

✻ Brewed for 29s

❯ server is running crazy fast                                                  

● The tick rate lives in the world's storage, and the server app re-asserts the
  flake default (1000 ms) at every launch by pushing
  system.setTickDuration(1000) to the CLI port — its output tells you whether
  that landed: a "tick duration set: 1000 ms/tick" line, or a warning that the
  CLI never answered and the world kept its stored rate. Crazy-fast means the
  stored rate won — likely a leftover from an earlier world, with the assert
  failing to land this launch.

  Immediate fix from inside the VM:

  nix run .#cli
  system.setTickDuration(1000)

  and the pace settles on the next tick. If the launch output showed the warning
  ("CLI (21026) never answered"), tell me — that's the assert mechanism needing
  a look rather than a one-off.

❯ what are you doing, stop wasting time and focus                               

● Fetch(https://unpkg.com/@screeps/backend@3.3.0/lib/game/server.js)
  ⎿  Interrupted · What should Claude do instead?

❯ stop, what is it you think needs to happen? what do you think the problem is  

● The problem: the browser separates the viewer's origin (localhost:8080) from
  the server's (localhost:21025) and demands the server opt in with CORS
  headers. We loaded screepsmod-cors to provide them. The evidence from your
  last run says it loaded and produced nothing — the preflight came back
  headerless.

  What I think is going on: the mod attaches its middleware by listening for a
  hook (expressPreConfig) that the backend has to fire while building its HTTP
  app. If the backend version this server vendors (3.3.0) doesn't fire that
  hook, the mod loads, registers a listener that never fires, and adds nothing —
  exactly matching what you saw. The fetch you stopped was going to confirm or
  kill that by reading the backend's source. Unconfirmed either way.

  What I think needs to happen: stop depending on the server's cooperation
  entirely. Serve the viewer and the API from one origin — replace the client
  app's static file server with a small proxy that serves the viewer's files AND
  forwards /api and the websocket to 21025. The browser then talks only to
  :8080, same origin, and CORS ceases to exist as a concept in this system.
  That's the design the steamless clients use, it works regardless of backend
  internals or mod versions, and it removes the cors mod dependency and this
  whole class of failure — including for every reader who'd otherwise hit
  whatever we just hit.

  If you want the mod path confirmed first, the one fetch of backend 3.3.0's
  source settles it. My recommendation is the proxy.

  /socket/... by splicing the TCP streams. Result: the browser talks only to
  :8080.

  origin instead of :21025, so requests are same-origin through the proxy.

  built files, and apps.client runs node proxy.mjs instead of python's static
  server.
  4. Leave screepsmod-cors where it is — inert but harmless; removing it is
  separate housekeeping for your call.
  5. Record your new rule in workflow memory: announce intended actions before
  taking them.

✻ Baked for 23s

❯ i dont see a map, do I need to deploy first?                                  

● Deploy affects what's in the room, and it isn't required for the map itself —
  with no deploy the account owns nothing, so room discovery falls back to the
  world's start room, and the renderer should still draw that room's terrain
  plus its neutral objects (sources, controller) from the first socket snapshot.
  A deploy is what puts your spawn and creep in the picture and gives your
  account a room of its own.

  So "no map" needs one distinguishing fact: what does the page show right now —
  still the connect form (with what status text under it), or the viewer layout
  (room name and tick counter in the corner) with a blank canvas? And any red
  lines in the browser console (F12)? That tells us whether it's stuck before
  the renderer or inside it.

● The script's own message names the gap: no credentials anywhere —
  secrets/SCREEPS_LOCAL_CREDS doesn't exist on this branch (only SCREEPS_TOKEN
  does). Quickest path is the env-var form, with any username/password you
  choose — this world is fresh, and the script registers the account itself:

  SCREEPS_LOCAL_EMAIL=mlitchard SCREEPS_LOCAL_PASSWORD=<pick one> nix run
  .#deploy-local

  future runs, the durable form is the secrix file the message mentions, created
  from the dev shell: secrix create secrets/SCREEPS_LOCAL_CREDS -i 
  "$SCREEPS_IDENTITY" -r "$(cat $SCREEPS_IDENTITY.pub)" with username:password
  as the content — but the env-var run gets you deployed right now.

❯ this makes no sense, i already logged in with credentials                     

● Two different doors, same key needed at each. The credentials you typed into
  the browser form went to the viewer page and live only in that page — nothing
  put them where the deploy script looks. deploy-local is a shell script; its
  documented sources are the two in its error message: the
  SCREEPS_LOCAL_EMAIL/SCREEPS_LOCAL_PASSWORD environment variables, or the
  encrypted secrets/SCREEPS_LOCAL_CREDS file — and this branch has neither.

  So use the same pair that just worked in the viewer:

  SCREEPS_LOCAL_EMAIL=<the username you logged in with>
  SCREEPS_LOCAL_PASSWORD=<same password> nix run .#deploy-local

  Same account, code deployed onto it, spawn placed — and the viewer session you
  already have watches it happen.

  Expansion section) — which is how !3y414… turned into cd dox-populi/…. Single
  quotes suppress it:

● The probe explains the 401 rather than wasting it: the server just answered
  ok:1 for lambdafan with the string from your deploy command — because

  the 401.

  browser, sign in with lambdafan and the deploy-command string, and set the new

❯ you already know. the same way it was working before. why do you think the

● You're right — that flow already exists and I pointed you away from it.
  deploy-local is the credential-setter, same as on main: when signin fails, it
  pushes the password you gave it through the server CLI and retries, so
  whatever pair you hand it becomes the account's pair. Nobody ever sets a
  password anywhere else.

  So: rerun it with the password you intend (single-quoted, for the ! problem):

  SCREEPS_LOCAL_EMAIL=lambdafan SCREEPS_LOCAL_PASSWORD='<the password you mean>'

  That run makes your intended pair the real one, and the viewer login takes it
  from then on.

● Sequence taken as given: deploy-local ran clean with the password you intend,
  then the viewer login with that same pair returned 401.

  The one mechanism that exactly fits "it worked before, I changed the password,
  now the same form gives 401": the browser saved your earlier login and is
  autofilling the old password into the form — the field looks filled, you press
  connect, and the stale string goes to the server. The form's autocomplete is
  set to exactly the mode browsers autofill.

  Two steps, in order:

  1. In the viewer form, click into the password field, clear it completely, and
  type the password by hand, then connect.
  2. If that still 401s, run this in the VM with your real pair — it asks the
  server directly, bypassing the browser entirely, and you don't paste the
  password anywhere I see:

  curl -sS -X POST http://127.0.0.1:21025/api/auth/signin -H 'Content-Type:
  application/json' --data '{"email":"lambdafan","password":"YOUR_PASSWORD"}'

  ok:1 there means the server holds your pair and the problem is in the form
  path; a failure there means the deploy run didn't set what we think it did,
  and the deploy command's exact quoting is the next thing to look at.

❯ why do you not have access in your shell                                      

● My shell runs each command detached from a terminal: the command executes,
  output comes back, and there's no channel for me to answer prompts
  mid-command. git add -p works by asking y/n per hunk on a live TTY — with no
  one on the other end, it would hang or bail. The harness states this limit for
  interactive git generally. Your !-prefixed commands run in your real terminal
  with you at the prompts, which is why the split is yours to drive.

✻ Baked for 17s

❯ we need one more step passing credtentials via env variable is unacceptable,  
  we need to verify project meets spec                                          

● The audit against your config law — env vars optional, never required — comes
  out clean everywhere except the exact spot you named:

  - server: STEAM_API_KEY, SCREEPS_HOST, SCREEPS_TICK_MS, SCREEPS_DATA_DIR — all
  optional with defaults (dummy key, loopback, flake tickMs, repo root).
  - deploy: SCREEPS_IDENTITY optional, defaults to your key path; token from
  secrets/SCREEPS_TOKEN.
  - viewer/run-vm/installer knobs — all optional overrides.
  - deploy-local: the precedence is right (env overrides the secrets file), but
  secrets/SCREEPS_LOCAL_CREDS doesn't exist on this branch, so the file-default
  path has been dead and env vars were effectively required. That's the
  violation.

  The fix is creating the secrets file — an act with your key, so it's yours.
  The non-interactive form from secrix's own help (stdin → encrypt):

  echo -n 'lambdafan:YOUR_PASSWORD' | nix run .#secrix encrypt
  secrets/SCREEPS_LOCAL_CREDS -- -r "$(cat /path/to/your/key.pub)"

  With that file in place, nix run .#deploy-local works bare — no env vars — and
  the env pair drops back to being the override the law allows. The file is
  age-encrypted like SCREEPS_TOKEN, so it commits; slot it into the server
  commit or its own, your sequencing call.
