  exists in the repo, on the other branch, and never entered this one's tree.

  The fix that reuses the solved artifact instead of minting anything:

  git checkout 1-tutorial-one -- secrets/SCREEPS_LOCAL_CREDS

  That brings the committed, encrypted file into this branch's tree — same
  credentials you decrypted before, deploy-local finds it, env vars become
  optional overrides, and the spec is met. Want me to run it, or are you taking
  it?

● Nothing is lost — the commit just needs unwinding. git commit -a swept every
  tracked change (and the files staged earlier, server-log.txt and
  .claude/settings.local.json among them) into 75e67ef. One command takes it
  apart while keeping every change in the working tree:

  git reset HEAD~1

  That removes the commit and unstages everything; the files stay exactly as
  they are. Then the split runs as planned: git add -p flake.nix (yes to server
  hunks, no to the two viewer hunks), add the mods lockfiles and
  secrets/SCREEPS_LOCAL_CREDS, commit the server message; then add flake.nix,
  viewer/, docs/viewer-contract.md, commit the viewer message — leaving
  .gitignore, settings.local.json, and server-log.txt uncommitted.

  One check before you run it: this assumes 75e67ef hasn't been pushed. If it
  has, say so and the unwind changes shape.

✻ Churned for 8s

❯ what does 'n' mean?                                                           
● In git add -p, each hunk gets a verdict: y stages it (it goes into the next
  commit), n skips it — the change stays in your working tree, unstaged,
  untouched. Nothing is discarded either way; "n" just means "this one waits."
  The skipped viewer hunks get picked up later by the plain git add flake.nix
  before the second commit. (q quits the session, ? prints git's own legend for
  the rest.)

✻ Worked for 7s

❯ we are all good. we now how the problems that come with success. primarily,   
  we have a fuckton of new material to make another blog with. the blog         
  throughline is accessibility                                                  

● That's real material, and it has something the other posts don't: a build
  where the accessibility requirement forced original engineering instead of
  adaptation. What the record now holds for it:

  - The requirement, stated by you in one line each: the purchase must go; "it
  must all work in the VM or it doesn't work"; "the same behavior I have always
  had." Those three sentences are the post's spine — accessibility defined as:
  nothing to buy, nothing on the host but QEMU, and no degraded experience.
  - The search that came up empty: both steamless clients relocate the purchase
  (their own READMEs say so), every open browser client traces to one 2019
  codebase with mouse interaction never implemented, and the one complete client
  is an unlicensed host-side Unity app. The official renderer sits open-sourced
  with its images, explicitly blessed for third-party clients — and the sweep
  found nothing that actually consumes it. The gap was real.
  - The build: the viewer — contract research from published artifacts, the
  same-origin proxy that made CORS stop existing, and the game's own onboarding
  rebuilt: respawn behind a confirm, click a tile to place your spawn, starting
  in a room where the click succeeds.
  - The debugging story, honestly recorded: the crash-looping backend caught by
  reading the server log, the export-shape archaeology that ended when your
  "rely on documentation instead of deduction" ruling landed, the status bar
  that turned silent failures into named ones. Your corrections are part of the
  record and they steer the whole thing.
  - The evidence: transcripts (this session, once exported), server logs, the
  sweep's verdict table, and a witness path a reader can run.

  Open questions for the discussion, whenever you want them: whether this is a
  second off-series post or takes a numbered place, and where the seam falls
  between it and the tutorial-one post — the VM machinery currently sits in that
  one's story, and the server/viewer work grew out of the same accessibility
  push.

✻ Cooked for 24s

❯ I think the process has revealed that tutorials can have multiple blogs       
  attached to them. so maybe the off series blog are forming a hyper text. one  
  particular hypertext path will result in different experiences. for example,  
  someone who wants to get to the point will have a click through path that     
  fulfills that. someone who wants all the meanderings get that, and anywhere   
  in between. so maybe each tutorial is a hypertext not a linear text           

● The structure fits how the work actually happened. Tutorial one already
  produced three distinct stories — the port, the record repair, the
  accessibility build — and forcing them into a line would falsify their
  relationship: they didn't happen in sequence, they happened around the same
  stage. A hypertext with the tutorial as its spine lets each page stay one
  honest story, and the paths do the sequencing per reader.

  What the existing machinery already gives you: the anchor is solid — every
  reader stands on a branch and an ISO, so wherever their click-path wanders,
  their feet are on one stage. The Promise sections already work as junctions
  ("what you get here"); the objectives-up-front law means a reader entering any
  page mid-path knows immediately whether it serves them. And the
  get-to-the-point path already exists implicitly: promise → equipment →
  exercises, skipping every session narrative.

  What the idea demands that doesn't exist yet: the links have to carry intent,
  on the page. A bare link makes a maze; a labeled one makes a path — "just want
  it running: continue to X" against "how this was actually fought out: detour
  to Y." That labeling is editorial work per page, and it's where the different
  experiences you're describing actually get constructed. The other discipline:
  every page keeps answering which tutorial it serves, so the graph stays a set
  of tutorials with texture and never becomes a homepage of essays.

  For the material at hand it resolves the seam question I raised: the
  accessibility post becomes the second page hanging off tutorial one, and the
  tutorial-one page links to it at exactly the moment the purchase problem
  appears in its story. Fast-path readers never see it; meander readers get the
  whole fight, corrections included.

✻ Brewed for 26s
